Boundedness and Morawetz estimates on subextremal Kerr–de Sitter
Georgios Mavrogiannis

Abstract
. We study the Klein–Gordon equation □ g a , M , l ​ ψ − μ KG 2 ​ ψ = 0 \Box_{g_{a,M,l}}\psi-\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}\psi=0 on subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole backgrounds with parameters ( a , M , l ) (a,M,l) , where l 2 = 3 Λ l^{2}=\frac{3}{\Lambda} . We prove boundedness and Morawetz estimates assuming an appropriate mode stability statement for real frequency solutions of Carter’s radial ode. Our results in particular apply in the very slowly rotating case | a | ≪ M , l |a|\ll M,l , and in the case where the solution ψ \psi is axisymmetric. This generalizes the work of Dafermos–Rodnianski [ 14 ] on Schwarzschild–de Sitter. The boundedness and Morawetz results of the present paper will be used in our companion [ 43 ] to prove a ‘relatively non-degenerate integrated estimate’ for subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black holes (and as a consequence exponential decay). In a forthcoming paper [ 42 ] , this will immediately yield nonlinear stability results for quasilinear wave equations on subextremal Kerr–de Sitter backgrounds.



1.
Introduction

A fundamental class of solutions of Einstein’s equation with a positive cosmological constant
\Lambda>0
, in the absence of matter

(1.1)

Ric[g]-\Lambda g=0,\qquad\Lambda>0

is the Kerr–de Sitter family of black hole solutions

(1.2)

(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M,l}),

see
[
7
,
8
]
. These are the analogues of the Kerr black holes, which solve (
1.1
) with
\Lambda=0
. The parameters
(a,M,l)
represent respectively the angular momentum per unit mass
a
, the mass
M
of the black hole and a quantity related to the cosmological constant
\Lambda>0
via

(1.3)

l\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\sqrt{\frac{3}{\Lambda}}.

The spacetime metric (
1.2
), in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
, takes the form

(1.4)

\displaystyle g_{a,M,l}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta}dr^{2}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta_{\theta}}d\theta^{2}+\frac{\Delta_{\theta}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-\Delta a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Xi^{2}\rho^{2}}\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2}-2\frac{\Delta_{\theta}(r^{2}+a^{2})-\Delta}{\Xi\rho^{2}}a\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi dt

\displaystyle-\frac{\Delta-\Delta_{\theta}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\rho^{2}}dt^{2},

see already Section
2.3
, with
\Delta_{\theta}=1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\cos^{2}\theta,\penalty\ \rho^{2}=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,\penalty\ \Xi=1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}
, where

(1.5)

\Delta(r)=(r^{2}+a^{2})\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)-2Mr.

Note that in the non-rotating case
a=0
the metric (
1.4
) reduces to the Schwarzschild–de Sitter metric

(1.6)

g_{M,\Lambda}=-\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}\right)dt^{2}+\frac{1}{1-\frac{2M}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}},

where
d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
is the standard metric of the unit sphere.

In this paper we revisit the study of the Klein–Gordon equation

(1.7)

\Box_{g_{a,M,l}}\psi-\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}\psi=0,

with mass
\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}\geq 0
(the case
\mu_{\textit{KG}}=0
is called the ‘wave equation’ and the case
\mu^{2}_{KG}=\frac{2\Lambda}{3}
the conformally coupled case) on a subextremal Kerr–de Sitter spacetime. We call the Kerr–de Sitter black hole triad
(a,M,l)

subextremal
, when the polynomial
\Delta
, see (
1.5
), attains four distinct real roots, see already Definition
2.2
. The Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
) has been extensively studied in the case
\Lambda\geq 0
in recent years
[
53
,
17
,
19
,
18
,
20
,
46
,
52
,
50
,
44
,
58
,
60
,
4
]
. For the study of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
) on
\Lambda<0
spacetimes, see
[
37
,
31
,
35
,
36
,
59
]
.

We prove boundedness and degenerate Morawetz estimates (Theorem
1.4
) for solutions of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
) with mass
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
on a subextremal Kerr–de Sitter spacetime, with parameters
(a,M,l)
, under the following condition:

\displaystyle\text{(MS):\penalty\ mode stability on the real axis for Carter's radial ode holds on a curve in subextremal}

\displaystyle\text{parameter space connecting}\penalty\ (a,M)\penalty\ \text{to the subextremal Schwarzschild--de\penalty\ Sitter family.}

We will show moreover that (MS) holds in the very slowly rotating case
|a|\ll M,l
. This generalizes the work of Dafermos–Rodnianski
[
14
]
on Schwarzschild–de Sitter
(a=0)
and is based on the framework of
[
17
]
which proved boundedness and Morawetz estimates for Kerr
(\Lambda=0)
. In the special case where
\psi
is axisymmetric our results hold for
(a,M,l)
in the full subextremal range.

To prove Theorem
1.4
we follow the strategy of
[
17
]
, namely we reduce the problem to constructing suitable fixed frequency energy multipliers for Carter’s ode, see already (
1.12
). Two major difficulties are superradiance and trapping. We note that in the Kerr–de Sitter case, for certain black hole parameters, the spacetime admits trapping that corresponds to
\omega=0
,
|m|\gg 1
frequencies, unlike the asymptotically flat Kerr case where trapping occurs only for frequencies
|\omega|\gg 1
. To reduce the problem to an analysis of real frequencies, we also appeal to a continuity argument in the parameters
a,M
starting from Schwarzschild–de Sitter
a=0
, see already Section
9
(this explains the precise form of the (MS) assumption).
In contrast to the
\Lambda=0
case, it is not known if (MS) holds for all subextremal black hole parameters
(a,M,l)
, say for the important special cases
\mu_{KG}^{2}=0
or
\mu_{KG}^{2}=\frac{2\Lambda}{3}
. For general
\mu^{2}_{KG}>0
, then (MS) is not expected to hold for all subextremal
(a,M,l)
, see
[
53
]
for a mode instability result in the
\Lambda=0
case.

In our companion
[
43
]
, we prove a ‘relatively non-degenerate’ integrated energy estimate for solutions of (
1.7
) under the condition (MS). For this, we introduce a novel operator
\mathcal{G}
, for the entire subextremal range of Kerr–de Sitter black holes, thus generalizing our previous work
[
44
]
on Schwarzschild–de Sitter, and we use this commutation in conjunction with the result of Theorem
1
. Note that the commutation with
\mathcal{G}
had been previously studied by Holzegel–Kauffman in the
\Lambda=0
Schwarzschild case, see
[
32
]
, to treat the wave equation with small first order error terms. Moreover, see the recent work
[
33
]
where the authors define a related
\mathcal{G}
commutation for the asymptotically flat
\Lambda=0
Kerr black hole. An immediate Corollary of the ‘relatively non-degenerate’ integrated estimate of our
[
43
]
is exponential decay for solutions of equation (
1.7
).

In our forthcoming
[
42
]
, we use
[
43
]
to give an elementary proof of stability results for quasilinear wave equations on Kerr–de Sitter black hole backgrounds, assuming only (MS).

1.1.
Previous results on Kerr(–de Sitter) backgrounds

We briefly mention some previous results for linear and non-linear waves on black hole backgrounds. For a more complete discussion, see our previous
[
44
,
45
]
.

Concerning linear results, in addition to
[
14
]
to be discussed extensively in Section
1.2
, note the proof of exponential decay for solutions of the wave equation on Schwarzschild–de Sitter away from the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
, by Bony–Häfner
[
5
]
, and the proof of exponential decay for the solutions of the wave equation on Kerr–de Sitter up to and including the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
, by Dyatlov
[
19
,
18
]
. Moreover, we refer the reader to the following additional linear results
[
5
,
4
,
19
,
18
,
58
,
60
,
17
,
62
,
54
,
56
,
40
,
22
]
and references therein. See especially the more recent work of Petersen–Vasy
[
48
]
, as well as the partial mode stability result on Kerr–de Sitter of Casals–Teixeira da Costa
[
10
]
and the recent work of Hintz
[
26
]
, which will be mentioned later in the paper. For a numerical study of modes on Kerr–de Sitter spacetime see the work
[
63
]
, where in fact the authors did not find any growing mode solutions in the conformal case
\mu^{2}_{KG}=\frac{2\Lambda}{3}
for the full subextremal range.

Concerning non-linear results, note first the remarkable proof by Hintz–Vasy of non-linear stability of the slowly rotating Kerr–de Sitter black hole
[
30
]
and the more recent result of Fang
[
21
]
. Moreover, for the study of non-linear wave equations on
\Lambda>0
spacetimes we refer the reader to
[
30
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
45
]
, and for non-linear wave equations on
\Lambda=0
spacetimes we refer the reader to
[
12
,
38
,
39
,
13
,
24
]
and references therein.

Finally, for the study of the wave equation on extremal black holes see the works of Aretakis and Gajic
[
2
,
3
,
23
]
. Moreover, for mode stability on extremal Kerr spacetimes see work of Teixeira da Costa
[
57
]
.

1.2.
Review of
[
14
]
and
[
17
]

The present paper is a generalization of the results of Dafermos–Rodnianski
[
14
]
on Schwarzschild–de Sitter and also uses the strategy of proof of Dafermos–Rodnianski–Shlapentokh-Rothman on Kerr
[
17
]
. We review these results here.

1.2.1.
The Morawetz (degenerate integrated decay) estimate of
[
14
]
on Schwarzschild–de Sitter

We define the following system of ‘special’ hyperboloidal coordinates

(1.8)

(\bar{t},r,\theta,\phi)

that relate to the usual Schwarzschild–de Sitter coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\phi)
as follows:

(1.9)

\bar{t}=t-\int_{3M}^{r}\frac{\xi(r)}{1-\frac{2M}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}},\qquad\xi(r)=\frac{1-\frac{3M}{r}}{\sqrt{1-9M^{2}\Lambda}}\sqrt{1+\frac{6M}{r}}.

For the definitions of the coordinates (
1.8
) also see our previous
[
44
]
. Moreover, note that
\{\bar{t}=\tau\}
, for
\tau\geq 0
, are spacelike hypersurfaces that connect the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
and foliate the causal past of the horizons
\mathcal{H}^{+},\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
. The specific choice of the coordinates (
1.8
) is also intimately connected to the nature of trapping at
r=3M
. Note that at
r=3M
we have
g(\partial_{\bar{t}},\partial_{r})=0
.

In
[
14
]
, Dafermos–Rodnianski studied the solutions
\psi
of the wave equation ((
1.7
) with
\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}=0
) and proved the following Morawetz type (degenerate integrated decay) energy estimate

(1.10)

\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}d\tau^{\prime}\int_{\{\bar{t}=\tau^{\prime}\}}(\partial_{r}\psi)^{2}+\left(1-\frac{3M}{r}\right)^{2}\left((\partial_{\bar{t}}\psi)^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}\right)\lesssim\int_{\{\bar{t}=\tau_{1}\}}(\partial_{\bar{t}}\psi)^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi)^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2},

for all
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
, where
\not{\nabla}
is the covariant derivative of the fixed
r
-spheres. The degeneration of (
1.10
) at the photon sphere
r=3M
is necessitated by the obstructions of
[
55
,
51
]
. Remarkably, the Morawetz estimate (
1.10
) does not degenerate at the horizon, due to the redshift effect, which can be captured by a multiplier current, see
[
15
]
.

Faster than any polynomial decay for solutions of the wave equation on a Schwarzschild–de Sitter background is an immediate corollary of the Morawetz estimate (
1.10
) suitable commutations and an iterative argument, see
[
17
]
. (We note, however, that exponential decay for solutions in Sobolev spaces does not follow immediately from (
1.10
).)

1.2.2.
The Morawetz estimate of
[
17
]
on
\Lambda=0
Kerr

We here review the proof of the Morawetz estimate for the wave equation on subextremal Kerr exteriors of
[
17
]
.

In
[
17
]
the authors used Carter’s separation of variables for the wave operator on Kerr, see
[
6
]
. Specifically, let
r^{\star}
be the tortoise coordinate, see
[
17
,
16
]
, and
{}^{\prime}=\frac{d}{dr^{\star}}
. Carter’s radial ode reads

(1.11)

u^{\prime\prime}+\left(\omega^{2}-V(r^{\star},\omega,m,\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell})\right)u=H,

where
\omega
denotes the time frequency,
m
denotes the azimuthal frequency and
\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}
denotes a frequency associated with the eigenvalues of the angular part of Carter’s separation, where
\ell\geq|m|
. Note that in
[
17
]
the error term
H
is associated with a time cut-off.

The paper
[
17
]
divided the frequency space
\{(\omega,m,\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell})\}
into frequency regimes and constructed energy currents for the ode (
1.11
) in each of these regimes in order to prove frequency localised energy estimates. To treat the non-stationary bounded frequencies (
|\omega|,\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\sim 1
), the authors utilized a flux bound on the horizon, which holds for the entire subextremal family of Kerr black holes. They inferred this bound from a quantitative version of mode stability on the real axis
\imaginary\omega=0
, proved by Shlapentokh-Rothman
[
54
]
. (See already Proposition
6.7
for such an estimate in the setting of the present paper, given the assumption (MS).) For the original proof of mode stability on the upper half plane
\imaginary\omega>0
see the seminal work of Whiting
[
62
]
.

To justify the frequency analysis of
[
17
]
, based entirely on real frequencies
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
, the authors appealed to a novel continuity argument in the rotation parameter
a
. Namely, the authors first proved their Morawetz estimate for ‘
future integrable
’ solutions of the wave equation, for which the Fourier inversion formula holds. Then, by utilizing a continuity argument in the rotation parameter
a
(starting from
a=0
, for which the result indeed holds) they proved that all solutions of the wave equation that arise from compactly supported initial data are indeed future integrable and therefore the Morawetz estimate of
[
17
]
holds for all solutions, in the full subextremal range
|a|<M
.

Similarly to the estimate (
1.10
), the Morawetz estimate of
[
17
]
degenerates appropriately at an interval around
r=3M
, but does not degenerate on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, exploiting the redshift effect
[
15
]
. However, in contrast to estimate (
1.10
), the Morawetz estimate of
[
17
]
exhibits appropriate weights in
r
in the fluxes and spacetime integrals to capture the correct behavior at null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

1.3.
Preliminaries for the statement and proof of Theorem
1

Before presenting the rough version of our main Theorem
1
, and discussing the proof, see already Section
1.4
, we first need several preliminaries.

1.3.1.
Carter’s radial ode in Kerr–de Sitter

Following
[
6
,
35
]
, we apply a cut-off to the solution
\psi
of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
) with mass
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
and then decompose into real Fourier modes and then into spheroidal harmonics. As in the
\Lambda=0
case, Carter’s separation of variables for the wave operator implies that the radial part
u
of the separated solution, for the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
), satisfies the ode

(1.12)

u^{\prime\prime}+(\omega^{2}-V)u=H

where
{}^{\prime}=\frac{d}{dr^{\star}}
, and
r^{\star}
is the tortoise coordinate, see Section
2.5
. Here

(1.13)

\omega\in\mathbb{R},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z}

are the Fourier frequencies with respect to the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
t,\varphi
respectively, see already Section
6.5
, and the potential
V
is real and takes the form

(1.14)

V=V_{0}+V_{\textit{SL}}+V_{\mu_{\textit{KG}}},\qquad V_{0}-\omega^{2}=\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}\left(\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}+(a\omega)^{2}-2m\omega a\Xi\right)-\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)^{2},

where
V_{\textit{SL}},V_{\mu_{\textit{KG}}}
are frequency independent terms (the latter is associated with the Klein–Gordon mass) see Section
6.5
, and
\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
are the eigenvalues of the spheroidal harmonics, which for convenience are indexed by
\ell
, where
\ell\geq|m|
. The inhomogeneous term
H
on the RHS of (
1.12
) comes from the cut-off.

As we shall see below the Fourier space decomposition allows us to capture the phenomena of trapping and superadiance at the level of modes and moreover allows us to rigorously formulate our mode stability condition (MS).

1.3.2.
An energy identity for the solution
u

Let

(1.15)

r_{+},\qquad\bar{r}_{+}

be the two largest roots of the polynomial
\Delta
, see (
1.5
), which correspond to the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
of Kerr–de Sitter respectively, see already the Penrose–Carter diagram of Figure
1
. We have that
\lim_{r\rightarrow r_{+}}r^{\star}(r)=-\infty
and
\lim_{r\rightarrow\bar{r}_{+}}r^{\star}(r)=+\infty
, see already Section
2.5
.

We multiply the radial ode (
1.12
) with
\bar{u}
and after integration by parts we obtain

(1.16)

\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)|u|^{2}(r^{\star}=+\infty)+\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)|u|^{2}(r^{\star}=-\infty)=\imaginary(\bar{u}H).

To derive the above we are using that the smoothness of the solution
\psi
and the geometry of the cut-off implies the following

(1.17)

u^{\prime}=-i\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)u,\qquad u^{\prime}=i\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)u,

at
r^{\star}=-\infty,+\infty
respectively, see already (
6.28
).

1.3.3.
Superradiant frequencies and mode stability

Let

(1.18)

K_{+}=\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi},\qquad\bar{K}_{+}=\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi}

be the Hawking–Reall vector fields associated with the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
respectively, see already Section
2.8
. (Note that in the
\Lambda=0
Kerr limit we have that
\bar{K}_{+}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \partial_{t}
since in the Kerr limit
\bar{r}_{+}=+\infty
, see already Lemma
3.2
.)

An explicit computation shows that the
\bar{K}_{+}
energy flux along the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the
\bar{K}^{+}
flux along the cosmological horizon
\bar{H}^{+}
are respectively given by

(1.19)

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}K_{+}\psi\overline{\bar{K}_{+}\psi}=\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\left(\partial_{t}\psi+\omega_{+}\partial_{\varphi}\psi\right)\cdot\overline{\left(\partial_{t}\psi+\bar{\omega}_{+}\partial_{\varphi}\psi\right)},\qquad\int_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}}|\bar{K}_{+}\psi|^{2}=\int_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}}\left|\partial_{t}\psi+\bar{\omega}_{+}\partial_{\varphi}\psi\right|^{2}.

In particular, if we consider a solution of the form
\psi=e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\varphi}\psi_{0}(r,\theta)
then the expressions (
1.19
) have different signs when the following expression is negative

(1.20)

\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right).

Therefore, we define the superradiant frequencies on Kerr–de Sitter to be

(1.21)

\mathcal{SF}=\{(\omega,m):\penalty\ am\omega\in\left(\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\}

or equivalently
\mathcal{SF}=\{(\omega,m):\penalty\ \left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)<0\}
. We note that if
a=0
then
\mathcal{SF}=\emptyset
. Moreover, we note that for any
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
we have
(\omega,0)\notin\mathcal{SF}
; i.e. axisymmetric frequencies are not superradiant.

For the superradiant frequencies (
1.21
), the left hand side of the energy identity (
1.16
) is not coercive. As a result, for such frequencies, we cannot conclude that “mode stability holds on the real axis”, i.e. the absence of non trivial real frequency solutions of (
1.12
) with
H=0
and satisfying the boundary conditions (
1.17
). To make this more precise, we denote as
\mathcal{W}(\omega,m,\ell,\mu^{2}_{KG})
the Wronskian associated with ingoing and outgoing solutions, at the event
\mathcal{H^{+}}
and cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
respectively, of the homogeneous
(H=0)
radial ode (
1.12
) of Kerr–de Sitter, see already Section
6.8
. Note that

(1.22)

\mathcal{W}(a,M,l,\mu_{KG},\omega,m,\ell)=0

where
\omega\neq\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}m,\penalty\ \omega\neq\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}m,\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z},\ell\geq|m|
, precisely when there exists a nontrivial mode solution with freqencies
\omega,m,\ell
. Thus, mode stability on the real axis is equivalent to the statement that (
1.22
) does not hold for any
\omega\neq\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}m,\penalty\ \omega\neq\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}m,\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z},\ell\geq|m|
. Note however that for the case
\omega=m=0
there do exist trivial mode solutions that correspond to constant solutions of the wave equation (
1.7
) (with
\mu_{KG}=0
). (Furthermore, we treat the near borderline superradiant frequencies
0\leq|\omega-\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}m|\ll 1
and
0\leq|\omega-\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}m|\ll 1
with the estimate of Proposition
16.6
, where we immediately obtain that there are no real mode solutions.)

It is an open problem to prove mode stability for the wave equation,
\mu^{2}_{KG}=0
, or the conformal wave equation,
\mu^{2}_{KG}=\frac{2\Lambda}{3}
, on Kerr–de Sitter in the spirit of
[
62
,
54
]
. One does not expect mode stability to hold for general mass
\mu^{2}_{KG}>0
, see the work on the Klein–Gordon equation on asymptotically flat Kerr
[
53
]
. However, note the partial mode stability result of Casals–Teixeira da Costa
[
10
]
and the recent result of Hintz
[
26
]
, which we will refer to later.

1.4.
The rough version of Theorem
1

The reader familiar with the Carter–Penrose diagrammatic representation may wish to refer to Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
Carter–Penrose diagram of Kerr–de Sitter

In the present paper we are concerned with the region

(1.23)

\{r_{+}\leq r\leq\bar{r}_{+}\}.

We will fix a system of coordinates

(1.24)

(t^{\star},r,\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})\in\mathbb{R}_{t^{\star}}\times[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]_{r}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{(\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})}

which we term modified Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates (see already Section
2
for their definition) covering (
1.23
). Moreover, for any
\tau\geq 0
the set

(1.25)

\{t^{\star}=\tau\}

will be a Cauchy hypersurface for
\{t^{\star}\geq\tau\}
that connects the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
. Also, let

(1.26)

n

denote the future unit normal to the foliation
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
.

We present the rough version of our boundedness and Morawetz estimate, where for the detailed version of Theorem
1
see already Section
7
.

Theorem
1

(rough version)
.

Let
l>0
,
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
and let
(a,M,l)
be subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole parameters. Moreover, assume that the following condition holds

\displaystyle\text{(MS):\penalty\ mode stability on the real axis for Carter's radial ode\penalty\ \eqref{eq: subsec: sec: intro, subsec 2, eq 1} holds on a curve in subextremal}

\displaystyle\text{parameter space connecting}\penalty\ (a,M)\penalty\ \text{to the subextremal Schwarzschild--de\penalty\ Sitter family}.

Let
\psi
be a solution of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
). Then, we have the following energy estimates

(1.27)

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}d\tau^{\prime}\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau^{\prime}\}}\mu^{2}_{KG}|\psi|^{2}+|Z^{\star}\psi|^{2}+\zeta_{\textit{trap}}(r)\big(|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+(\partial_{t^{\star}}\psi)^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}|Z^{\star}\psi|^{2}+|\partial_{t}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+\mu^{2}_{KG}|\psi|^{2},

(1.28)

\displaystyle\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}}|Z^{\star}\psi|^{2}+|\partial_{t^{\star}}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+\mu^{2}_{KG}|\psi|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}|Z^{\star}\psi|^{2}+|\partial_{t}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+\mu^{2}_{KG}|\psi|^{2},

\displaystyle\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}}|Z^{\star}\psi|^{2}+|\partial_{t}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+|\psi|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}|Z^{\star}\psi|^{2}+|\partial_{t}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+|\psi|^{2},

for all
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
, where
Z^{\star}
is a regular spacelike vector field on
\{t^{\star}\geq 0\}
that extends smoothly on the horizons where it is linearly independent from the generators, and
\zeta_{\textit{trap}}(r)=\left(1-1_{[R^{-},R^{+}]}\right)\left(1-\frac{\frac{R^{-}+R^{+}}{2}}{r}\right)^{2}
, where
[R^{-},R^{+}]\subset(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
and
R^{-}=R^{+}=3M
for
a=0
.

Moreover, if the solution of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
) is axisymmetric
\partial_{\varphi^{\star}}\psi=0
, then we also have the estimates (
1.27
), (
1.28
), with
R^{-}=R^{+}
, without assuming the condition (MS).

Note the following remarks

Remark 1.1
.

We emphasize that the condition (MS) concerns only Carter’s homogeneous fixed frequency radial ode (
1.12
) for real frequencies
(\omega,m,\ell)\in\mathbb{R}\times\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\{m\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq|m|}
. It does not depend thus on the completeness properties of the angular eigenvalues for complex frequencies.

Remark 1.2
.

By Proposition
6.6
, for any fixed
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
, the set of black hole parameters for which (MS) holds includes
a=0
. Moreover, by Proposition
9.3
, this set is open. Therefore, (MS) in particular holds in the slowly rotating case
|a|\ll M,l
, where the constant implicit in
\ll
depends only on
\mu_{KG}
. Furthermore, in view of the recent work of Hintz
[
26
]
for
\mu^{2}_{KG}=0
, we have that (MS) also holds in the case
0\leq|a|<M\ll l
, where the constant depends on
M-|a|
.

Remark 1.3
.

We do not expect that the condition (MS) holds for all Klein–Gordon masses
\mu^{2}_{KG}>0
, see for example the construction
[
53
]
of real mode solutions for the Klein–Gordon equation in subextremal Kerr.

1.5.
Discussion of the proof

We give some comments about the proof, highlighting especially some differences from
[
17
]
.

1.5.1.
The de Sitter frequencies and trapping

If the ergoregion is connected and non-empty, namely when

(1.29)

\max_{r\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]}\frac{\Delta}{a^{2}}\leq 1,\quad|a|\not{=}0,

where for
\Delta
see (
1.5
), then there exists a complete
\partial_{t}
orthogonal trapped null geodesic, and it never intersects the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
or the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
(see Appendix
B
). This geometric phenomenon is not present in the
\Lambda=0
case, where note that in
[
17
]
the authors prove that in fact there can be no
\partial_{t}
orthogonal trapped null geodesic, for any subextremal black hole parameters.

This phenomenon can be seen at the fixed frequency analysis of (
1.12
) near
\omega=0
, for high azimuthal frequencies
|m|\gg 1
, where trapping again corresponds to a maximum of the potential
V
, see (
1.14
), such that
\max V\approx\omega^{2}
. This is in sharp contrast to the Kerr case, see
[
17
]
, where trapping takes place only for large time frequencies
|\omega|\gg 1
. Specifically, the existence of a
\partial_{t}
orthogonal geodesic for appropriate Kerr–de Sitter black hole parameters, see Section
B
, necessitates that our fixed frequency estimates in the ‘de Sitter frequencies’ :

(1.30)

\mathcal{DSF}=\Biggl\{(\omega,m):\penalty\ |\omega|\in\left[0,\frac{|am|\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right]\Biggr\}

will degenerate appropriately. See Section
16.3
for estimates in this frequency range.

The interior of the frequency set (
1.30
) is empty in the
\Lambda=0
Kerr case. Moreover, the interior of the frequency set (
1.30
) is empty in the Schwarzschild de Sitter case if
a=0
. In the very slowly rotating case
|a|\ll M,l
, we note that there is no trapping in this range.

1.5.2.
The continuity argument

As mentioned earlier, since we rely entirely on real frequency analysis
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
, the proof of Theorem
1
relies on a continuity argument in the black hole parameters, see Section
9
, which is inspired by the relevant continuity argument of
[
17
]
.

Note that the set of subextremal black hole parameters for which mode stability on the real axis holds may fail to be connected for given
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
, since mode stability for the full subextremal family has not been proven in the Kerr-de Sitter case. Therefore, in the condition (MS) we must restrict to black hole parameters for which mode stability on the real axis holds on an entire curve in parameter space connecting
(a,M)
to the Schwarzschild de Sitter family. See Definition
7.1
.

1.5.3.
Constant solutions when
\mu^{2}_{KG}=0

In the case
\mu^{2}_{KG}=0
constant functions are solutions of the wave equation (
1.7
). Thus, in this case one does not have integrated decay for zeroth order terms, see (
1.27
), and fixed frequency estimates near
\omega=0
(when
m=0
) take on a different form, see Section
16
, from the relevant ode estimate of
[
17
]
.

To generate zeroth order boundary terms on future hypersurfaces, see the second estimate of (
1.28
), we use currents for twisted derivatives, see Section
4.4
.

1.6.
Outline

We here present an outline of the structure of our paper.

In Section
2
we give all the necessary background for the Kerr–de Sitter spacetime, including the coordinates we use, the ergoregion of Kerr–de Sitter, volume forms, spacetime domains and hypersurfaces, and a useful global causal vector field.

In Section
3
we gather properties of the
\Delta
polynomial, which we use throughout the paper.

In Section
4
we give all the necessary background on the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
) on a Kerr–de Sitter black hole.

In Section
5
we construct the redshift vector fields and prove redshift estimates.

In Section
6
we present Carter’s separation of variables in Kerr–de Sitter. Specifically, we discuss in detail the angular ode, the eigenvalues of the angular ode, the radial ode, the necessary Parseval identities, and the Wronskian of the radial ode. This allows us to formulate (MS).

In Section
7
we present the detailed version of our main Theorem
1
.

In Section
8
we prove Theorem
8.1
, which concerns an inhomogeneous Morawetz estimate for sufficiently integrable functions in the full subextremal parameter range, without the restriction (MS), but with an extra horizon flux term on the right hand side. Essential in the proof of Theorem
8.1
is the fixed frequency Theorem
8.3
. The proof of the fixed frequency Theorem
8.3
is deferred, howerer, to Section
16
.

In Section
9
we prove Theorem
9.1
with the use of a continuity argument. Specifically, Theorem
9.1
states that, under the restriction (MS), all solutions of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
) are in fact ‘future integrable’.

In Section
10
we prove the Morawetz estimate of our main Theorem
1
, by combining Corollary
8.2
and Theorem
9.1
.

In Section
11
we prove the boundedness estimates of our main Theorem
1
.

In Section
12
we prove the Morawetz and boundedness estimates of our main Theorem
1
, for axisymmetric solutions of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
), for the full subextremal range without the restriction (MS).

The Sections
13
,
14
,
15
are auxilliary in the proof of the fixed frequency Theorem
8.3
, which is proved in Section
16
. Specifically, in Section
13
we study the critical points of the potential
V_{0}
. In Section
14
we present fixed frequency currents. In Section
15
we define the frequency regimes. In Section
16
we finally prove the fixed frequency Theorem
8.3
, as a Corollary of a more general statement, see Theorem
16.1
. Specifically, to prove Theorem
16.1
, we integrate appropriately chosen currents as in Section
14
in the frequency regimes of Section
15
. The estimates of Section
16
should be thought of as the heart of our argument.

In Appendix
A
we prove that the set of subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole parameters
\mathcal{B}_{l}
, for any
l>0
, is connected.

In Appendix
B
we prove that for appropriate subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole parameters
(a,M,l)
there exist trapped null and
\partial_{t}
-orthogogal geodesics.

1.7.
Acknowledgments

The author would like to thank his PhD supervisor M. Dafermos for introducing him to this problem and for his invaluable suggestions and comments throughout the author’s PhD studies.

A preliminary version of the results of the current paper were outlined in my thesis
[
41
]
, while the author was a PhD student at DPMMS Cambridge and a resident of Fitzwilliam College Cambridge.

2.
The Kerr–de Sitter spacetime

We define the background manifold and the black hole horizons.

Definition 2.1
.

We define the ‘fixed’ coordinates

(2.1)

(y^{\star},t^{\star},\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})

on the manifold
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
. We define the event horizon and the cosmological horizon respectively as

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{+}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{y^{\star}=0\},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{y^{\star}=1\}.

We define the following Kerr–de Sitter background manifold with boundary

(2.3)

\mathcal{M}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu[0,1]_{y^{\star}}\times\mathbb{R}_{t^{\star}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{(\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})}.

2.1.
The
\Delta
polynomial and the subextremal parameters

Let
(a,M,l)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We define the function

(2.4)

\Delta(r)\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\left(r^{2}+a^{2}\right)\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)-2Mr,

viewed as a polynomial in
r\in\mathbb{R}
, also see Figure
2
.

Figure 2.
The
\Delta
polynomial with 4 real roots

We next define the subextremal set of Kerr–de Sitter black hole parameters.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
l>0
. We define the open set

(2.5)

\mathcal{B}_{l}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{(a,M)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty):\penalty\ \Delta\penalty\ \textrm{attains four distinct real roots}\}.

If
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
we say that the black hole parameters
(a,M,l)
correspond to a subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole and denote as

(2.6)

\bar{r}_{-}(a,M,l)\quad<\quad 0\quad\leq\quad r_{-}(a,M,l)\quad<\quad r_{+}(a,M,l)\quad<\quad\bar{r}_{+}(a,M,l)

the four distinct real roots of the
\Delta
polynomial. Note that in the Schwarzschild–de Sitter case
(a=0)
we have
r_{-}=0
.

Note the following lemma

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
l>0
. Then, the set
\mathcal{B}_{l}
, see Definition
2.2
, is connected.

Proof.

See Appendix
A
.
∎

2.2.
Modified Kerr–de Sitter-star coordinates

We define the modified Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates, which cover the entire background manifold
\mathcal{M}
of Definition
2.1
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. We choose a smooth map

(2.7)

\displaystyle r:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathcal{B}_{l}\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow[r_{+}(a,M,l),\bar{r}_{+}(a,M,l)]

\displaystyle(l,a,M,y^{\star})

\displaystyle\mapsto r(a,M,l,y^{\star})\in[r_{+}(a,M,l),\bar{r}_{+}(a,M,l)],

such that for fixed
l,a,M
the map is a diffeomorphism
[0,1]\rightarrow[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]
. Then, we define the modified Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates to be

(2.8)

(r,t^{\star},\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star}).

The Kerr–de Sitter manifold
\mathcal{M}
, of Definition
2.1
, can now be written as

(2.9)

\mathcal{M}=[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]_{r}\times\mathbb{R}_{t^{\star}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{(\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})},

where

(2.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{+}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{r=r_{+}\},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{r=\bar{r}_{+}\}.

We have the following remarks

Remark 2.1
.

Note from Definition
2.1
that the Kerr–de Sitter star vector fields
\partial_{t^{\star}},\partial_{\varphi^{\star}}
are independent of the black hole parameters
(a,M,l)
.

Remark 2.2
.

Note that the leaves

(2.11)

\{t^{\star}=c\}

of the modified Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates of Definition
2.3
connect the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
. Moreover, note that the leaves
\{t^{\star}=c\}
are independent of the black hole parameters
(a,M,l)
. This will be useful later in the continuity argument, see already Section
9
.

2.3.
Boyer–Lindquist coordinates

We need the following definition

Definition 2.4
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Then, we define

(2.12)

\displaystyle\rho^{2}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}

\displaystyle r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,\qquad\Delta_{\theta}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\cos^{2}\theta,\qquad\Xi\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}.

We define the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates

Definition 2.5
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Moreover, let
\epsilon_{BL}(a,M,l)>0
be sufficiently small. We define the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates

(2.13)

(t,r,\theta,\varphi)

on
\mathcal{M}^{o}
as follows

(2.14)

t=t^{\star}+A(r;l,a,M),\qquad\varphi=\varphi^{\star}+B(r;l,a,M)\mod{2\pi},\qquad\theta=\theta^{\star}.

Here,
A(r),B(r)
satisfy

(2.15)

\displaystyle\frac{dA}{dr}=-\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta},\qquad\frac{dB}{dr}

\displaystyle=-\frac{a\Xi}{\Delta},\qquad r\in(r_{+},r_{+}+\epsilon_{BL}),

\displaystyle\frac{dA}{dr}=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta},\qquad\frac{dB}{dr}

\displaystyle=\frac{a\Xi}{\Delta},\qquad r\in(\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{BL},\bar{r}_{+}),

and we extend
A(r(y^{\star});l,a,M),B(r(y^{\star});l,a,M)
to
(0,1)\times\bigcup_{l>0}\left(\{l\}\times\mathcal{B}_{l}\right)
in a smooth manner such that the hypersurfaces
\{t^{\star}=c\}
are spacelike with respect to the metric
g_{a,M,l}
, see already Definition
2.6
.

We now define the Kerr–de Sitter metric in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates to be

Definition 2.6
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. We define the Kerr–de Sitter metric as

(2.16)

\displaystyle g_{a,M,l}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta}dr^{2}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta_{\theta}}d\theta^{2}+\frac{\Delta_{\theta}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-\Delta a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Xi^{2}\rho^{2}}\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2}-2\frac{\Delta_{\theta}(r^{2}+a^{2})-\Delta}{\Xi\rho^{2}}a\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi dt

\displaystyle-\frac{\Delta-\Delta_{\theta}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\rho^{2}}dt^{2},

where
\Delta
has been defined in (
1.5
), and also see Definition
2.4
for
\Xi,\rho^{2},\Delta_{\theta}
. The metric (
2.16
) extends smoothly to
\mathcal{M}
and moreover the metric depends smoothly on
a,M
for fixed
l
.

Note that the vector field
W=\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi}
is timelike away from the horizons, see already Lemma
2.2
. We time orient
\mathcal{M}
by the vector
W
.

In view of the form of the metric
g_{a,M,l}
, we may deduce that the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
are null hypersurfaces and moreover are the future boundaries of the manifold
\mathcal{M}
.

Remark 2.3
.

Note the identities
\partial_{t}=\partial_{t^{\star}},\partial_{\varphi}=\partial_{\varphi^{\star}}
.

2.4.
The ergoregion in Kerr–de Sitter

We define the ergoregion of the Kerr–de Sitter spacetime to be

(2.17)

\textit{Ergo}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{g(\partial_{t},\partial_{t})>0\}=\{\Delta-\Delta_{\theta}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta<0\}.

Remark 2.4
.

In Section
B
we will prove that
\partial_{t}
orthogonal trapping takes place in Kerr–de Sitter if and only if the ergoregion is connected and non empty, namely when

(2.18)

\max_{r\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]}\frac{\Delta}{a^{2}}\leq 1,\quad|a|\not{=}0.

2.5.
Tortoise coordinate

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. We define the tortoise coordinate
r^{\star}:(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})\rightarrow(-\infty,+\infty)
as follows

(2.19)

\frac{dr^{\star}}{dr}=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta(r)},

where for
\Delta(r)
see (
2.4
), with
r^{\star}(r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}})=0
, where

(2.20)

r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}}\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})

is the unique local maximum of

(2.21)

\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}},

see already Lemma
3.3
. We note that
\lim_{r\rightarrow r_{+}}r^{\star}(r)=-\infty
and
\lim_{r\rightarrow\bar{r}_{+}}r^{\star}(r)=+\infty
, and moreover note that
r^{\star}(r)
is a diffeomorphism mapping
(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})\rightarrow(-\infty,+\infty)
.

We will often use the primed notation

(2.22)

{}^{\prime}=\frac{d}{dr^{\star}}

for the
r^{\star}
-derivative. Finally, for a value
\alpha\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
, we will often use the notation

(2.23)

\alpha^{\star}=r^{\star}(\alpha).

2.6.
The regular vector field
Z^{\star}

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Let
\partial_{r}|_{star}
be the coordinate vector field of the modified Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates
(t^{\star},r,\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})
, see Definition
2.3
, and
\partial_{r}|_{\textit{BL}}
be the coordinate vector field of the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
, see Definition
2.5
. We will define here a hybrid vector field.

For a sufficiently small

(2.24)

\epsilon_{\textit{hyb}}(a,M,l)>0,

we choose a smooth cut-off that satisfies

(2.25)

\chi_{\textit{hyb}}(r)=\begin{cases}0,\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu&r\in(r_{+},r_{+}+\epsilon_{\textit{hyb}})\cup(\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{\textit{hyb}},\bar{r}_{+}),\\
1,\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu&r\in(r_{+}+2\epsilon_{\textit{hyb}},\bar{r}_{+}-2\epsilon_{\textit{hyb}}).\end{cases}

Then, we define the hybrid vector field

(2.26)

Z^{\star}=(1-\chi_{\textit{hyb}}(r))\partial_{r}|_{\textit{star}}+\chi_{\textit{hyb}}(r)\partial_{r}|_{\textit{BL}}.

It is easy to see from the Definitions
2.3
,
2.5
of the Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates and the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates respectively and from the definition of the Kerr–de Sitter metric, see Definition
2.6
, that for a sufficiently small
\epsilon_{hyb}(a,M,l)>0
, the vector field
Z^{\star}
is spacelike. Also see the relevant computation of
[
19
]
.

The vector field
Z^{\star}
coincides with the tortoise coordinate vector field
\partial_{r}|_{BL}
, of Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, in the region
r\in[R^{-},R^{+}]
, where for
R^{\pm}
see Theorem
1
.

The hybrid vector field (
2.26
) is translation invariant and regular on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and on the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
. The significance of
Z^{\star}
will be that the derivatives
(Z^{\star}\psi)^{2}
do
not
degenerate in the integrand of the Morawetz estimate (
1.27
) of Theorem
1
.

2.7.
Spacelike hypersurfaces and their normal vector fields

A prototype spacelike hypersurface that connects the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
is

(2.27)

\{t^{\star}=0\}.

For any
\tau\geq 0
the hypersurfaces

(2.28)

\{t^{\star}=\tau\}

are Cauchy for their future and connect the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
. We denote as

(2.29)

n_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}

the future directed unit normal of the hypersurfaces
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
, which we often simply denote as

(2.30)

n.

2.8.
Hawking–Reall vector fields

We define the null normals of the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
respectively as the Hawking–Reall vector fields

(2.31)

K=\partial_{t^{\star}}+\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi^{\star}},\qquad\bar{K}=\partial_{t^{\star}}+\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi^{\star}}.

When there is no risk of confusion with (
2.30
) we will denote the normal vector fields (
2.31
) simply as

(2.32)

n.

2.9.
Causal domains

We define certain causal spacetime domains.

Definition 2.7
.

We define

(2.33)

D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{\tau_{1}\leq t^{\star}\leq\tau_{2}\},\qquad D(\tau_{1},\infty)\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{t^{\star}\geq\tau_{1}\},

where for the hypersurfaces
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
see Section
2.7
.

We here extend the domain of Definition
2.7
.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small. We define the extended manifold

(2.34)

\mathcal{M}_{\delta}=[r_{+}-\delta,\bar{r}_{+}+\delta]_{r}\times\mathbb{R}_{t^{\star}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{(\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})},

with respect to the Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates
(t^{\star},r,\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})
see Section
4.4
. The Kerr–de Sitter metric (
2.16
) extends smoothly to
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
by its analytic expression near
r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}
in Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates. Note that the hypersurfaces
\{t^{\star}=c\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
are spacelike for sufficiently small
\delta>0
. We define the spacelike boundaries of the manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
to be

(2.35)

\mathcal{H}^{+}_{\delta}=\{r=r_{+}-\delta,\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mut^{\star}>-\infty\},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}_{\delta}=\{r=\bar{r}_{+}+\delta,\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mut^{\star}>-\infty\}.

Now, we define the extended causal domains

(2.36)

D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{\tau_{1}\leq t^{\star}\leq\tau_{2}\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\delta},\qquad D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},+\infty)\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{t^{\star}\geq\tau_{1}\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\delta}.

2.10.
The induced metric on the
r
-sphere and its covariant derivative

We use

(2.37)

\not{g},\qquad\not{\nabla},\qquad d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}

to denote respectively the induced
g_{a,M,l}
metric on the
\mathbb{S}^{2}
factors of
\mathcal{M}
, the covariant derivative with respect to
\not{g}
and the standard metric of the unit sphere.

2.11.
Volume forms and notation

The spacetime volume form is

(2.38)

dg=v(r,\theta)dt^{\star}drd\not{g}=\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}v(r,\theta)dt^{\star}dr^{\star}d\not{g},

and
d\not{g}=v(r,\theta)\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Xi}\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi
with
v(r,\theta)\sim 1
, where for
\Delta,\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\rho^{2}=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta
see (
2.4
) and Definition
2.4
respectively.

We denote the induced volume form of the spacelike hypersurface
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
as

(2.39)

dg_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}.

It can be characterised as the unique 3-form such that

(2.40)

dg=n_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}\wedge dg_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}},

where
n_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}
is the unit normal of the leaf
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
see Section
2.7
. Note that the volume form (
2.39
), can be written as

(2.41)

dg_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}=v(r,\theta)drd\theta^{\star}d\varphi^{\star},

with
v(r,\theta)\sim r\sin\theta
, where the constant in the similarity depends only on the black hole parameters
(a,M,l)
and does not degenerate in the Kerr limit
l\rightarrow+\infty
.

We denote the volume forms of the null hypersurfaces
\mathcal{H}^{+},\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
respectively as

(2.42)

dg_{\mathcal{H}^{+}},\qquad dg_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}}

where these are defined to be the unique 3-forms such that

(2.43)

dg=(K^{+})^{\flat}\wedge dg_{\mathcal{H}^{+}},\qquad dg=(\bar{K}^{+})^{\flat}\wedge dg_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}},

where
\flat
here is the flat-musical isomorphism with respect to the Kerr–de Sitter metric, see (
2.16
), and
K,\bar{K}
are the Hawking–Reall vector fields of Section
2.8
.

From this point onward, when we integrate over the hypersurfaces

(2.44)

\{t^{\star}=\tau\},\qquad\mathcal{H}^{+},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}

the volume forms (
2.39
), (
2.42
) respectively are to be understood, if no volume form is explicitly denoted. Similarly, when we integrate over spacetime the volume form (
2.38
) is to be understood, if no volume form is explicitly denoted.

2.12.
Coarea formula

Let
f
be a continuous non-negative function. Then, note that the following holds

(2.45)

\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\frac{1}{r}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mufdg\sim\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}d\tau\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}fdg_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}},

where for the volume forms of the integrals of (
2.45
) see Section
2.11
. (Note that the constants in the similarity of inequality (
2.45
), with the inclusion of the
\frac{1}{r}
factor, do not degenerate in the Kerr limit
l=\infty
.)

2.13.
The future oriented vector field
W

Note the following Lemma

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. The vector field

(2.46)

W\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\partial_{t^{\star}}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi^{\star}},

is timelike in
\{r_{+}<r<\bar{r}_{+}\}
and null on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and on the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
.

Proof.

Consider a vector field of the form

(2.47)

\partial_{t^{\star}}+f(r)\partial_{\varphi^{\star}},

for a smooth
f(r)
. We compute the following

(2.48)

\displaystyle g(\partial_{t}+f(r)\partial_{\varphi},\partial_{t}+f(r)\partial_{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\left(-(\Delta-\Delta_{\theta}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta)-\frac{2}{\Xi}f(r)(\Delta_{\theta}(r^{2}+a^{2})-\Delta)a\sin^{2}\theta+\frac{1}{\Xi^{2}}f^{2}(r)\left(\Delta_{\theta}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-\Delta a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right)\sin^{2}\theta\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\left(\Delta+\sin^{2}\theta\left(\Delta_{\theta}\left(a-\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Xi}f(r)\right)^{2}+\frac{2}{\Xi}f(r)\Delta a-\frac{1}{\Xi^{2}}f^{2}(r)\Delta a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\left(-\Delta+\sin^{2}\theta\frac{2}{\Xi}f(r)\Delta a+\sin^{2}\theta\left(\Delta_{\theta}\left(a-\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Xi}f(r)\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{\Xi^{2}}f^{2}(r)\Delta a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right)\right),

where
\rho^{2}=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta
,
\Delta_{\theta}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\cos^{2}\theta
, see Definition
2.4
.
Therefore, for

(2.49)

f(r)=\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}},

equation (
2.48
) implies

(2.50)

\displaystyle g(\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi},\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi})=\frac{\Delta}{\rho^{2}}\left(-1+\sin^{2}\theta\frac{2a^{2}}{r^{2}+a^{2}}-\sin^{2}\theta\left(\frac{1}{\Xi^{2}}\left(\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)^{2}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right)\right).

We note

(2.51)

r_{+}>|a|,

see already Lemma
3.2
, and therefore, for
r\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]
the following holds

(2.52)

-1+\sin^{2}\theta\frac{2}{\Xi}\frac{a^{2}\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}=-1+\frac{2a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}<0.

Therefore, indeed the right hand side of equation (
2.50
) is negative in
(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
and zero where
r=r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}
.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

Note that our proof of Lemma
2.2
also holds for any
\Lambda=0
Kerr black hole, simply by substituting
\Xi=1

(l=+\infty)
in the proof.

Recall that in Definition
2.6
we chose the time orientation so that the vector field
W
, of the above Lemma
2.2
, is future oriented.

3.
Properties of the
\Delta
polynomial

We need the following Lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Then, the following hold:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\frac{d\Delta}{dr}=2r-\frac{4}{l^{2}}r^{3}-a^{2}\frac{2}{l^{2}}r-2M,\qquad\frac{d^{2}\Delta}{dr^{2}}=2-a^{2}\frac{2}{l^{2}}-\frac{12}{l^{2}}r^{2},\qquad\frac{d^{3}\Delta}{dr^{3}}=-\frac{24}{l^{2}}r,

(3.2)

\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r_{+})>0,\quad\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(\bar{r}_{+})<0,\quad\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}\right)(r_{+})>0,\quad\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}\right)(\bar{r}_{+})<0,

where for
\Delta
see (
2.4
).

Proof.

The identities (
3.1
) follow in a straightforward manner. The inequalities (
3.2
) follow immediately from the subextremality condition of Definition
2.2
.
∎

We need the following lemma

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. We have the following

(3.3)

\bar{r}_{-}<0<r_{-}<r_{+}<\bar{r}_{+},

(3.4)

M<r_{+}<\bar{r}_{+}<l,

(3.5)

r_{+}>|a|,

(3.6)

\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}<\frac{1}{4},

(3.7)

\Big|\frac{a}{M}\Big|<\frac{12}{10}.

Finally,

(3.8)

\bar{r}_{+}^{2}>\frac{1}{7}l^{2}

and therefore
\bar{r}_{+}\rightarrow\infty
for
l\rightarrow+\infty
(
\Lambda\rightarrow 0
).

Proof.

We prove (
3.3
). Note from Lemma
3.1
, that the following hold

(3.9)

\displaystyle\Delta(0)=a^{2}>0,\qquad\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(0)=-2M<0,\qquad\frac{d^{2}\Delta}{dr^{2}}(0)=2-2\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}>0.

Now, we note that
\frac{d^{2}\Delta}{dr^{2}}
attains exactly two roots, see Lemma
3.1
. For a point
r_{1}\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
such that

(3.10)

\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r_{1})<0

we obtain

(3.11)

\frac{d^{2}\Delta}{dr^{2}}(r_{1})\leq 0.

Therefore,

(3.12)

r_{-}>0

follows from (
3.9
). Therefore, we obtain

(3.13)

\bar{r}_{-}=-\left(r_{-}+r_{+}+\bar{r}_{+}\right)<0.

We now prove (
3.4
). Note that the inequality
\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r_{+})>0
implies

(3.14)

2(r_{+}-M)>\frac{4}{l^{2}}r_{+}^{3}+a^{2}\frac{2}{l^{2}}r_{+}>0

from which we obtain

(3.15)

r_{+}>M,

since
r_{+}>0
. Finally, the following holds

(3.16)

(\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2})(1-\frac{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}}{l^{2}})-2M\bar{r}_{+}=0

from which the following holds
(\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2})(1-\frac{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}}{l^{2}})>0
and therefore

(3.17)

|\bar{r}_{+}|<l,

which concludes the result.

Now, we prove (
3.5
). We write the polynomial
\Delta
in the form

(3.18)

\Delta=-\frac{1}{l^{2}}(r-\bar{r}_{-})(r-r_{-})(r-r_{+})(r-\bar{r}_{+}),

then by using the definition of
\Delta
, see (
2.4
), and by matching the relevant powers of
r
, we obtain the following

(3.19)

\bar{r}_{-}+r_{-}+r_{+}+\bar{r}_{+}=0,

(3.20)

\bar{r}_{-}r_{-}r_{+}\bar{r}_{+}=-l^{2}a^{2},

(3.21)

r_{+}\bar{r}_{+}+r_{-}r_{+}+r_{-}\bar{r}_{+}+\bar{r}_{-}r_{+}+\bar{r}_{-}\bar{r}_{+}+r_{-}\bar{r}_{-}=-l^{2}+a^{2}.

We use (
3.19
) in conjunction with (
3.21
) to obtain

(3.22)

a^{2}+r_{+}^{2}+\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+r_{-}^{2}+r_{-}r_{+}+r_{+}\bar{r}_{+}+\bar{r}_{+}r_{-}=l^{2}.

By using (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) we get

(3.23)

r_{-}r_{+}\bar{r}_{+}(r_{-}+r_{+}+\bar{r}_{+})=l^{2}a^{2},

which, in conjunction with (
3.22
), implies

(3.24)

l^{2}a^{2}<r_{-}r_{+}l^{2}<l^{2}r_{+}^{2},

from which (
3.5
) is immediate.

Now, we prove (
3.6
). We use equation (
3.22
), in conjunction with the bounds

(3.25)

\bar{r}_{+}>r_{+}>|a|,\qquad r_{-}>0

to obtain

(3.26)

a^{2}+a^{2}+a^{2}+a^{2}+\left(r_{-}^{2}+r_{-}r_{+}+\bar{r}_{+}r_{-}\right)<l^{2}

which indeed implies (
3.6
).

Now, we prove (
3.7
). The necessary and sufficient conditions on the coefficients of a quartic polynomial to attain four distinct real roots have been studied extensively, see for example
[
49
]
. From
[
49
]
we note that
\Delta
attains exactly 4 distinct real roots if and only if the following two inequalities hold

(3.27)

\displaystyle-256\left(\frac{a^{6}}{l^{6}}\right)-128\left(\frac{a^{4}}{l^{4}}\right)\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)^{2}+576\left(\frac{M^{2}a^{2}}{l^{4}}\right)\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle-432\frac{M^{4}}{l^{4}}-16\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)^{4}+16\frac{M^{2}}{l^{2}}\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)^{3}>0,

(3.28)

|a|<l.

We want to prove that if the black hole parameters satisfy

(3.29)

a=\alpha M,\qquad\alpha\geq\frac{12}{10}

then the
\Delta
polynomial attains at least one non-real root, and therefore the parameters do not correspond to a subexrtremal black hole. We substitute (
3.29
) in the inequality (
3.27
) and obtain

(3.30)

\displaystyle-16\alpha^{10}z^{10}-16(-1+\alpha^{2})z^{2}-48\alpha^{4}(11+2\alpha^{2})z^{6}-16\alpha^{6}(1+4\alpha^{2})z^{8}-16(27-33\alpha^{2}+4\alpha^{4})z^{4}>0

where

(3.31)

z=\frac{M}{l}.

We will prove that (
3.30
) cannot be satisfied for any
z\in\mathbb{R}
. It suffices to prove that the following part

(3.32)

\displaystyle-16(-1+\alpha^{2})z^{2}-16(27-33\alpha^{2}+4\alpha^{4})z^{4}-48\alpha^{4}(11+2\alpha^{2})z^{6}

\displaystyle=z^{2}\left(-16(-1+\alpha^{2})-16(27-33\alpha^{2}+4\alpha^{4})z^{2}-48\alpha^{4}(11+2\alpha^{2}))z^{4}\right),

of inequality (
3.30
), is negative for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
, since the remaining terms are negative. To prove that (
3.32
) is always negative, it suffices to prove that the polynomial

(3.33)

-16(-1+\alpha^{2})-16(27-33\alpha^{2}+4\alpha^{4})z^{2}-48\alpha^{4}(11+2\alpha^{2})z^{4}

is always negative. We first note that

(3.34)

256(AE)^{3}-128(ACE)^{2}+16AC^{4}E>0,\qquad 64A^{3}E-16(AC)^{2}>0

where

(3.35)

A=-48\alpha^{4}(11+2\alpha^{2}),\qquad C=-16(27-33\alpha^{2}+4\alpha^{4}),\qquad E=-16(-1+\alpha^{2}),

from which, by again appealing to (
3.27
), (
3.28
) (note the conditions of
[
49
]
) we obtain that the roots of the quartic polynomial (
3.33
) are never real. Moreover, we readily obtain that the polynomial (
3.33
) is negative at
0
, therefore it is always negative. Now, (since the remaining terms of (
3.30
) are also negative), we conclude that for a subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole we obtain

(3.36)

|a|<\frac{12}{10}M.

Finally, we prove (
3.8
). We use equation (
3.22
), together with the bounds
r_{-}<r_{+}<\bar{r}_{+}
to obtain

(3.37)

l^{2}<a^{2}+6\bar{r}_{+}^{2}<r_{+}^{2}+6\bar{r}_{+}^{2}<7\bar{r}_{+}^{2},

from which we conclude that
\bar{r}_{+}\rightarrow+\infty
as
l\rightarrow+\infty
.
∎

Remark 3.1
.

We note that

(3.38)

\sup_{l>0,(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}}\left|\frac{a}{M}\right|\in\left(1,\frac{12}{10}\right),

in contrast to the Kerr case where the extremal parameters are
\left|\frac{a}{M}\right|=1
. We will not pursue to compute the sharp upper bound of (
3.38
) as the bound
\frac{12}{10}
suffices for the purposes of this paper. Specifically, we only need the bound
|\frac{a}{M}|\leq\frac{12}{10}
in the proof of Lemma
13.1
.

The following Lemma will also be important

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Then, the function

(3.39)

\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}

attains exactly one critical point in
(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
, a maximum, which we denote as

(3.40)

r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}}.

Moreover, there exists a strictly positive constant
c(a,M,l)>0
, such that

(3.41)

\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}\left(\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}\right)(r=r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}})<-c(a,M,l)<0.

For sufficiently small rotation parameters
a
, there exists a constant
C(M,l)>0
, such that

(3.42)

|r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}}-3M|\leq C(M,l)a^{2}.

Proof.

First, we calculate the derivative

(3.43)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-2}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}}\left(r^{3}(1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}})-3Mr^{2}+r(a^{2}+\frac{a^{4}}{l^{2}})+Ma^{2}\right)=-2\frac{s(r)}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}},

where the polynomial

(3.44)

s(r)=r^{3}(1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}})-3Mr^{2}+r(a^{2}+\frac{a^{4}}{l^{2}})+Ma^{2}

enjoys the same roots as the function
\frac{d}{dr}\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}
, where we rewrite

(3.45)

\frac{d}{dr}\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}=\frac{\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-4r(r^{2}+a^{2})\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{4}}.

We infer that

(3.46)

s(r_{+})<0,\qquad s(\bar{r}_{+})>0,

see Lemma
3.1
for the derivatives of
\Delta
.

Moreover we note that

(3.47)

\frac{d^{2}s}{dr^{2}}(r)=6r(1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}})-6M=6(r-M)+6r\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}>0,\qquad r\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]

since
r_{+}>M
, see Lemma
3.2
.

Therefore, from (
3.46
), (
3.47
), we conclude that
s(r)
attains exactly one critical point in
[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]
, a maximum, which we denote as

(3.48)

r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}}.

Now, we proceed to prove (
3.41
). A straightforward computation shows that

(3.49)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})\frac{d\Delta}{dr}-4r\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}}\Bigg(-\Xi r^{3}+3Mr^{2}+\left(-a^{2}\Xi\right)r+\left(-Ma^{2}\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\dot{=}\frac{2}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\textit{pol}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu(r).

Note that

(3.50)

\frac{d\textit{pol}}{dr}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu(r)=-3\Xi r^{2}+6Mr-a^{2}\Xi,

which is a polynomial of degree
2
and has discriminant

(3.51)

\text{discriminant of }\frac{d\textit{pol}}{dr}=(6M)^{2}-12a^{2}\Xi^{2}=\left(6M\right)^{2}\left(1-\frac{12a^{2}\Xi^{2}}{(6M)^{2}}\right).

By using that
|\frac{a}{M}|\leq\frac{12}{10},\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
, see Lemma
3.2
, we readily see that

(3.52)

\frac{12a^{2}\Xi^{2}}{(6M)^{2}}=\frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{a}{M}\right)^{2}\left(1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{12}{10}\right)^{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{4}\right)^{2}=\frac{3}{4}<1.

Therefore, the roots of
\frac{d\textit{pol}}{dr}
are real and distinct and therefore the zeros of

(3.53)

\frac{d}{dr}\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}

are real and distinct. Therefore, we obtain that

(3.54)

\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}\Big|_{r=r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}}}\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}<0.

Finally, we note from the definition of the polynomial
s(r)
, see Lemma
3.3
, that

(3.55)

s(r)=r^{2}(r-3M)+a^{2}\left(\frac{r^{3}}{l^{2}}+r+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}r^{2}+M\right),

from which we conclude

(3.56)

|r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}}-3M|\leq C(M,l)a^{2},

as desired.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

Note that in the Schwarzschild–de Sitter case
(a=0)
, Lemma
3.3
implies

(3.57)

r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}}=3M.

Finally, we note the following Lemma, which follows immediately from the subextremality conditions of Definition
2.2
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Then,
\Delta
attains exactly one critical point in
(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
, a maximum, which we denote as
r_{\Delta,\textit{max}}
.

4.
Preliminaries for the Klein–Gordon equation

4.1.
The energy momentum tensor and the divergence theorem

Definition 4.1
.

Let
g
be a smooth Lorentzian metric. The energy momentum tensor associated with a solution
\psi
of the inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equation

(4.1)

\Box_{g}\psi-\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}\psi=F,

where
F
is a sufficiently regular function, is defined to be the following:

(4.2)

\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[\psi]\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\partial_{\mu}\psi\partial_{\nu}\psi-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\Big(|\nabla\psi|_{g}^{2}+\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}|\psi|^{2}\Big),

where
\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}\geq 0
is the Klein–Gordon mass and
|\nabla\psi|^{2}_{g}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mug^{\gamma\delta}\partial_{\gamma}\psi\partial_{\delta}\psi
. The energy current, with respect to a vector field
X
, is defined as

(4.3)

J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[\psi]X^{\nu}.

Note that for
X,N
everywhere future directed causal vector fields, the following holds

(4.4)

J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]N^{\mu}=\mathbb{T}(X,N)[\psi]\geq 0.

The divergence of the energy current is

(4.5)

\nabla^{\mu}J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]=X(\psi)\cdot F+\mathrm{K}^{X}[\psi],

where

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}^{X}[\psi]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu^{(X)}\pi^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\mu}\psi\partial_{\nu}\psi-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}|\nabla\psi|^{2}\right)\pi^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}|\psi|^{2}\Tr\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu^{(X)}\pi,\qquad^{(X)}\pi^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla^{\mu}X^{\nu}+\nabla^{\nu}X^{\mu}\big).

The following is the divergence theorem.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. Let
\psi
satisfy the inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equation

(4.7)

\Box_{g}\psi-\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}\psi=F

and let
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
,
\tau\geq 0
, be the hypersurfaces of Section
2.7
.

Then, the following holds

(4.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}}J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+\int_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\mathrm{K}^{X}[\psi]

\displaystyle=\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}-\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}X\psi\cdot F,

for
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
. The identity (
4.8
) is to be understood with respect to the normals of Section
2.7
,
2.8
and the volume forms of Section
2.11
.

For a further study of currents related to partial differential equations, see the monograph of Christodoulou
[
11
]
.

4.2.
Hardy inequalities

We need the following Hardy inequalities.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose that
f
is a differentiable real function of compact support in
[1,\infty)
, then the following holds

(4.9)

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}|f|^{2}(x)dx\leq C\int_{1}^{\infty}x^{2}\left|\frac{df}{dx}\right|^{2}(x)dx.

Moreover, we note the following Hardy inequality

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose that
f
is a differentiable real function in the interval
[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]
, then the following holds

(4.10)

\int_{r_{+}}^{\bar{r}_{+}}|f|^{2}dr\leq C(\delta)\int_{r_{+}}^{\bar{r}_{+}}\left|\frac{df}{dr}\right|^{2}dr+C(\delta)\int_{r_{+}+\delta}^{\bar{r}_{+}-\delta}|f|^{2}dr

for sufficiently small
\delta>0
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward by integration by parts for the function
\chi\psi
, where the smooth cut-off
\chi
satisfies
\chi(r)=1
in sufficiently small neighborhoods of
r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}
and
\chi(r)=0
away.
∎

4.3.
Poincare–Wirtinger inequality

We present a classical Poincare–Wirtinger type inequality that is taylored to the setting of the present paper.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
l>0
and let
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu_{KG}^{2}\geq 0
. Moreover, assume that
\psi
is a smooth function on the manifold
\mathcal{M}
. Then, for any
\tau\geq 0
we have the following

(4.11)

\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}|\psi-\underline{\psi}(\tau)|^{2}\leq B\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}

where
\underline{\psi}(\tau)=\frac{1}{|\{t^{\star}=\tau\}|}\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}\psi
and
|\{t^{\star}=\tau\}|=\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}1\cdot dg_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}
with respect to the volume forms of Section
2.11
. The constant
B
in inequality (
4.11
) depends only on
|\{t^{\star}=\tau\}|=c
, where the constant
c
is independent of
\tau
.

4.4.
Twisted derivatives and the twisted energy momentum tensor

The twisted covariant derivative that will be defined in this Section has been used in a number of works, see
[
61
,
37
,
34
]
and references therein.

We define the twisting function

(4.12)

f(r)=e^{C_{twi}\cdot r}

where
C_{twi}>0
is an arbitrary positive constant. We define the
f
-twisted covariant derivatives as follows

(4.13)

\tilde{\nabla}_{\mu}(\cdot)=f\nabla_{\mu}\left((\cdot)\penalty\ \frac{1}{f}\right),\qquad\tilde{\nabla}_{\mu}^{\dagger}(\cdot)=-\frac{1}{f}\nabla_{\mu}\left((\cdot)\penalty\ f\right).

We need the following definition

Definition 4.2
.

Let
l>0
,
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. We define the
f
-twisted energy momentum tensor for the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
) as follows

(4.14)

\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{ab}[\psi]=\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\psi\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\psi-\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}\left(\tilde{\nabla}^{\alpha}\psi\tilde{\nabla}_{\alpha}\psi+V_{twi}|\psi|^{2}\right),\qquad V_{twi}=-\left(\frac{\Box_{g}f}{f}-\mu^{2}_{KG}\right).

Moreover, let
\psi
be a smooth solution of the inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equation
\Box\psi-\mu^{2}_{KG}\psi=F
. Then, for any smooth vector field
X
we define

(4.15)

\tilde{J}^{X}_{a}[\psi]=\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{ab}[\psi]X^{b},\qquad\tilde{\mathrm{K}}^{X}[\psi]:=\penalty\ ^{(X)}\pi_{ab}\tilde{\mathbb{T}}^{ab}[\psi]+X^{b}\tilde{S}_{b}[\psi],

where

(4.16)

\tilde{S}_{b}[\psi]=\frac{\tilde{\nabla}^{\dagger}_{b}(fV_{twi})}{2f}\psi^{2}+\frac{\tilde{\nabla}^{\dagger}_{b}f}{2f}\tilde{\nabla}_{\sigma}\psi\tilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}\psi.

These are thus examples of compatible currents in the sense of Christodoulou
[
11
]
.

For a general smooth
\psi
we have

(4.17)

\nabla_{a}\tilde{\mathbb{T}}^{a}\penalty\ _{b}[\psi]=\left(-\tilde{\nabla}^{\dagger}_{a}\tilde{\nabla}^{a}\psi-V_{twi}\cdot\psi\right)\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\psi+\tilde{S}_{b}[\psi].

Moreover, we compute

(4.18)

\nabla^{a}\tilde{J}_{a}^{X}[\psi]=F\cdot X(\psi)+\tilde{\mathrm{K}}^{X}[\psi].

Remark 4.1
.

Note that if
X
is one of the Killing vector fields

(4.19)

X=\partial_{t},\partial_{\varphi}

then we have

(4.20)

\tilde{\mathrm{K}}^{X}[\psi]=0.

We note the following lemma

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
l>0
,
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. Moreover, let
X,Y
be two smooth vector fields which are future directed timelike on
\{r_{+}<r<\bar{r}_{+}\}
. Furthermore, we make the assumption that on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
the vector fields
X,Y
are either timelike or equal to
\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi}
. Similarly, we make the assumption that on the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
the vector fields
X,Y
are either timelike or equal to
\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi}
.

Then, there exist strictly positive constants
c(a,M,l,X,Y)>0,C_{twi}(a,M,l,X,Y)>0
, independent of the Klein–Gordon mass
\mu_{KG}
, and we have that

(4.21)

\tilde{\mathbb{T}}[\psi](X,Y)+C_{twi}\cdot\mathbb{T}[\psi](X,Y)\geq c\cdot\Delta|\psi|^{2}+c\Delta\cdot\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=1}|\partial_{t}^{i_{1}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{2}}\not{\nabla}^{i_{3}}\psi|^{2}

for all
r\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]
.

Proof.

This is immediate after taking
C_{twi}(a,M,l,\mu_{KG})>0
sufficiently large, since
\mathbb{T}(X,Y)\gtrsim\Delta\cdot\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=1}|\partial_{t}^{i_{1}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{2}}\not{\nabla}^{i_{3}}\psi|^{2}
.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

We will use the twisted energy momentum tensor in Section
9.2
to control zeroth order future boundary terms of the form
\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}}|\psi|^{2}
, in the case
\mu_{KG}=0
.

5.
Red-shift and Superradiance

In this section we construct redshift vector field multipliers and then use them to derive redshift estimates.

5.1.
Red-shift multipliers

We present the redshift multipliers for the horizons, see Theorem 7.1 of the lecture notes
[
16
]
, and [
[
52
]
, Proposition
6.5
].

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
l>0
,
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. Then, there exist positive constants

(5.1)

c(a,M,l,\mu^{2}_{KG}),\qquad\bar{c}(a,M,l,\mu^{2}_{KG})>0,

there exists a parameter
\epsilon_{red}>0
sufficiently small satisfying

(5.2)

r_{+}(a,M,l)<r_{+}+2\epsilon_{red}(a,M,l)<\bar{r}_{+}-2\epsilon_{red}(a,M,l)<\bar{r}_{+},

and there exists a vector field

(5.3)

N

such that the following holds.

We have

\displaystyle N\text{ is timelike in }\{r<r_{+}+2\epsilon_{red}\}\cup\{\bar{r}_{+}-2\epsilon_{red}<r\},\qquad[N,\partial_{t}]=0,\qquad[N,\partial_{\varphi}]=0,

N=0,\quad\textit{for }\{r_{+}+2\epsilon_{red}\leq r\leq\bar{r}_{+}-2\epsilon_{red}\},

and for any sufficiently regular function
\psi
we have

(5.4)

\mathrm{K}^{N}[\psi]\geq cJ^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]N^{\mu},\quad\textit{for }\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon_{red}\},\qquad\mathrm{K}^{N}[\psi]\geq cJ^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]N^{\mu},\quad\textit{for }\{r\geq\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{red}\},

where for
\mathrm{K}^{X}
see Definition
4.1
. The result of the present Proposition also holds for the extended domain
D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
, see Definition
2.8
.

Proof.

The proof can be inferred from the lecture notes
[
16
]
or
[
52
]
. We present the basic steps.

First, we note the definition of the surface gravity of the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
respectively

(5.5)

\kappa_{+}=\frac{1}{2}|\frac{d\Delta}{dr}|,\qquad\bar{\kappa}_{+}=\frac{1}{2}|\frac{d\Delta}{dr}|.

In view of the subextremality condition
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
we note that

(5.6)

\kappa_{+},\qquad\bar{\kappa}_{+}>0.

There exist vector fields

(5.7)

Y^{+},\qquad\bar{Y}^{+}

that satisfy

(5.8)

\nabla_{Y^{+}}Y^{+}=-\sigma\left(K^{+}+Y^{+}\right),\qquad\nabla_{\bar{Y}^{+}}\bar{Y}^{+}=-\sigma\left(\bar{K}^{+}+\bar{Y}^{+}\right)

(5.9)

g(Y^{+},K^{+})=-2,\qquad g(\bar{Y}^{+},\bar{K}^{+})=-2

which are null on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
respectively, where for the Hawking–Reall vector fields
K^{+},\bar{K}^{+}
see Section
2.8
, and moreover
Y^{+},\bar{Y}^{+}
are invariant under pushforwards with respect to
\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{\varphi}
. The constant
\sigma>0
is to be chosen sufficiently large, see already (
5.14
).

We recall from Definition
4.1
that for a vector field
X
the following holds

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}^{X}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}\pi^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\mu}\psi\partial_{\nu}\psi-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}|\nabla\psi|^{2}_{g}\right)\pi^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}|\psi|^{2}\Tr\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu^{(X)}\pi.

Therefore, for a sufficiently large
\sigma>0
we note that

(5.11)

-\Tr\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu^{(Y^{+})}\pi>0,\qquad-\Tr\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu^{(\bar{Y}^{+})}\pi>0,

on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
respectively.

Let
\epsilon_{red}>0
be sufficiently small. Again, by following the lecture notes
[
16
]
we smoothly extend
N
such that it is timelike in
\{r<r_{+}+2\epsilon_{red}\}\cup\{\bar{r}_{+}-2\epsilon_{red}<r\}
and
\partial_{t},\partial_{\varphi}
invariant with

(5.12)

N=Y^{+}+K^{+}

in
\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon_{red}\}

(5.13)

N=\bar{Y}^{+}+\bar{K}^{+}

in
\{\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{red}\leq r\}
and
N=0
at
\{r_{+}+2\epsilon_{red}\leq r\leq\bar{r}_{+}-2\epsilon_{red}\}
.

Therefore, by following the arguments of the lecture notes
[
16
]
, in view of the positivity of the surface gravities (
5.6
), we take
\sigma>0
sufficiently large and conclude that the following hold

(5.14)

\mathrm{K}^{N}[\psi]\geq bJ^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]N^{\mu},\qquad\mathrm{K}^{N}[\psi]\geq bJ^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]N^{\mu},

in
\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon_{red}\},\{\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{red}\leq r\}
respectively. This completes the proof.
∎

The following Lemma will be very useful

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
l>0
,
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}\geq 0
. Let
\psi
be a smooth function such that
\partial_{\varphi}\psi=0
. Then, we have

(5.15)

\mathrm{K}^{W}[\psi]=0,\qquad\tilde{\mathrm{K}}^{W}[\psi]=0

where
W=\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi}
, see Lemma
2.2
.

Proof.

We denote
f(r)=\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}
. Then, we obtain

(5.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}^{\partial_{t^{\star}}+f(r)\partial_{\varphi}}[\psi]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{K}^{(f(r)\partial_{\varphi})}[\psi]=\frac{1}{2}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu^{(f\partial_{\varphi})}\pi_{\mu\nu}\mathbb{T}_{\mu_{KG=0}}^{\mu\nu}=\mathbb{T}^{\mu}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu_{\varphi}(\nabla f)_{\mu}=\mathbb{T}(\nabla f,\partial_{\varphi})[\psi]=0,

where we used

(5.17)

\Tr\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu^{(f\partial_{\varphi})}\pi=g^{\mu\nu}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu{}^{(f\partial_{\varphi})}\pi_{\mu\nu}=2g^{r\nu}\frac{df(r)}{dr}g(\partial_{\varphi},\partial_{\nu})=0,

to eliminate the Klein–Gordon term of the energy momentum tensor. The proof of
\tilde{\mathrm{K}}^{W}=0
is immediate from (
4.15
).
∎

5.2.
The redshift estimates

We will use the redshift multiplier
N
, of Proposition
5.1
, to obtain the following.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. Let
\epsilon_{red}>0
be as in Proposition
5.1
. Then, for all
j\geq 1
there exists a sufficiently small
\delta(a,M,l,\mu_{KG},j)>0
and there exists a positive constant

(5.18)

C=C(a,M,l,j)>0,

such that for all smooth solutions
\psi
of the inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equation

\Box_{g_{a,M,l}}\psi-\mu^{2}_{KG}\psi=F

where
F
is sufficiently regular function, we obtain

(5.19)

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq i\leq j-1}\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon_{red}\}}J^{N}_{\mu}[N^{i}\psi]N^{\mu}+\sum_{0\leq i\leq j-1}\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{red}\}}J^{N}_{\mu}[N^{i}\psi]N^{\mu}

\displaystyle+\sum_{0\leq i\leq j-1}\Bigg(\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}J^{N}_{\mu}[N^{i}\psi]n^{\mu}+\int_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}J^{N}_{\mu}[N^{i}\psi]n^{\mu}

\displaystyle+\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon_{red}\}}J^{N}_{\mu}[N^{i}\psi]n^{\mu}+\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}\cap\{r\geq\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{red}\}}J^{N}_{\mu}[N^{i}\psi]n^{\mu}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{0\leq i\leq j-1}\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[n^{i}\psi]n^{\mu}

\displaystyle+C\sum_{0\leq i\leq j-1}\Big(\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r_{+}+\epsilon_{red}\leq r\leq r_{+}+2\epsilon_{red}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[n^{i}\psi]n^{\mu}+\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{\bar{r}_{+}-2\epsilon_{red}\leq r\leq\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{red}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[n^{i}\psi]n^{\mu}\Big)

\displaystyle+C\sum_{1\leq i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\leq j-1}\Big(\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq r_{+}+2\epsilon_{red}\}}|\not{\nabla}^{i_{1}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{2}}\partial_{t}^{i_{3}}F|^{2}

\displaystyle+\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{\bar{r}_{+}-2\epsilon_{red}\leq r\leq\bar{r}_{+}\}}|\not{\nabla}^{i_{1}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{2}}\partial_{t}^{i_{3}}F|^{2}\Big),

for all
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
. The result of the present Proposition also holds replacing
D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
with the extended domain
D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
and
\mathcal{H}^{+},\bar{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
with
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{\delta},\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}_{\delta}
, see Definition
2.8
, where
\{t^{\star}=c\}
is now to be understood as a subset of
D_{\delta}
.

Proof.

From Proposition
5.1
we obtain immediately

(5.20)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}J^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}+\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon_{red}\}}J^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+\int_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}J^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}}+\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}\cap\{r\geq\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{red}\}}J^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}

\displaystyle+\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon_{red}\}}J^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]N^{\mu}+\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{red}\}}J^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]N^{\mu}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+C\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r_{+}+\epsilon_{red}\leq r\leq\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{red}\}}|\mathrm{K}^{N}[\psi]|

\displaystyle+C\Big(\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r_{+}+\epsilon_{red}\leq r\leq r_{+}+2\epsilon_{red}\}}|F|^{2}+\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{\bar{r}_{+}-2\epsilon_{red}\leq r\leq\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{red}\}}|F|^{2}\Big)

which easily implies (
5.19
) with
j=1
.

To obtain (
5.19
) for all orders
j\geq 1
we proceed as follows.

For
m=(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4})
, the following holds on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
:

(5.21)

\Box(Y^{k}\psi)-\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}Y^{k}\psi=\kappa_{k}Y^{k+1}\psi+\sum_{|m|\leq k+1,m_{4}\leq k}c_{m}E_{1}^{m_{1}}E_{2}^{m_{2}}L^{m_{3}}Y^{m_{4}}\psi+Y^{k}F,

where
(E_{1},E_{2},L,Y)
is a local null frame on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, with
Y=Y^{+}
, with
\kappa_{k}>0
, and
c_{m}
is a smooth function on the horizon such that
Y(c_{m})=0
. Note that an analogous identity (
5.21
) holds on the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
where
Y=\bar{Y}^{+}
, with
\bar{\kappa}_{k}>0
in the place of
\kappa_{k}
and a smooth
\bar{c}_{m}
in the place of
c_{m}
, where
\bar{Y}(\bar{c}_{m})=0
.

We apply the analogue of (
5.20
) to
Y^{k}\psi
. The positivity
\kappa_{k},\penalty\ \bar{\kappa}_{k}>0
and the structure of the remainder terms in (
5.21
) allows to conclude (
5.19
) inductively for any
j\geq 1
by standard elliptic estimates. See
[
16
]
for more details.
∎

5.3.
Boundedness in the axisymmetric case

We will use the following proposition for the axisymmetric case of Theorem
1
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. There exists a constant

(5.22)

C(a,M,l,\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}})>0,

where
C(a,M,l,\mu_{KG}^{2})
and the following holds.

Let
\psi
satisfy the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.7
) and also satisfy the axisymmetric condition

(5.23)

\partial_{\varphi^{\star}}\psi=0.

Then,

(5.24)

\displaystyle\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}}J_{\mu}^{n}[\psi]n^{\mu}
